Well… mostly I’m here because occupational health thought I should be checked out. They’re saying I’m not eating enough. I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most days I just have black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, and sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.

Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder because I just… I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nI know that may sound extreme. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just want to answer it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose objectively, yes. I mean, my uniform hangs off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, if that makes sense. When the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, part of me still thought, well, I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. Or at least I don’t see what other people seem to be seeing.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I tend to go straight to my stomach and my waist. If I don’t… it’s hard to think about much else. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re describing, where I can’t stop. I’m pretty controlled with food, honestly. If anything, I’d say I’m careful. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder because I just… I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nI know that may sound extreme. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just want to answer it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose objectively, yes. I mean, my uniform hangs off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, if that makes sense. When the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, part of me still thought, well, I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. Or at least I don’t see what other people seem to be seeing.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I tend to go straight to my stomach and my waist. If that looks… not lean enough, then it kind of colors the whole day. I know that probably sounds vain or unreasonable, but it’s more like I judge whether I can feel okay with myself based on that. Like whether I can respect myself that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is closely tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say it’s the only thing, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people tell me they see. I go straight to my stomach, my waist, whether I look lean enough. And if I don’t, it can make me feel like I’ve failed before the day’s even started.\n\nIt sounds a bit ridiculous saying it out loud, but yes… whether I can respect myself that day gets tied up in that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I’m here because occupational health thought I should be checked out. They’re saying I’m not eating enough. I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most days I just have black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, and sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not ideal. It’s just that a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat a full meal?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m terrified, exactly. But yes, I do get worried. If the scale is up even half a pound, I get this sort of jolt, like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back more, just to get back on track. I know that probably sounds excessive… I just really can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight most of the day?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big thing of it, but when I look in the mirror I don’t really see what other people say they see. I just zero in on my stomach and my waist, and then it sort of sets the tone for the day.\n\nIf I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself a bit. If I don’t… it’s hard to think about much else. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re describing, where I can’t stop. I’m pretty controlled with food, honestly. If anything, I’d say I’m careful. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. I… I know people say I’m underweight, and I’m not trying to argue with them exactly. They’re probably seeing something I’m not.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I’m here because occupational health thought I should be checked out. They’re saying I’m not eating enough. I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most days I just have black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, and sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not ideal. It’s just that a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat a full meal?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m terrified, exactly. But yes, I do get worried. If the scale is up even half a pound, I get this sort of jolt, like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back more, just to get back on track. I know that probably sounds excessive… I just really can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight most of the day?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big thing of it, but when I look in the mirror I don’t really see what other people say they see. I just zero in on my stomach and my waist, and then it sort of sets the tone for the day.\n\nIf I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself a bit. If I don’t… it’s hard to think about much else. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re describing, where I can’t stop. I’m pretty controlled with food, honestly. If anything, I’d say I’m careful. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder because I just… I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nI know that may sound extreme. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just want to answer it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose objectively, yes. I mean, my uniform hangs off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, if that makes sense. When the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, part of me still thought, well, I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. Or at least I don’t see what other people seem to be seeing.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I tend to go straight to my stomach and my waist. If that looks… not lean enough, then it kind of colors the whole day. I know that probably sounds vain or unreasonable, but it’s more like I judge whether I can feel okay with myself based on that. Like whether I can respect myself that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is closely tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say it’s the only thing, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people tell me they see. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I’m here because occupational health thought I should be checked out. They’re saying I’m not eating enough. I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most days I just have black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, and sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not ideal. It’s just that a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat a full meal?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m terrified, exactly. But yes, I do get worried. If the scale is up even half a pound, I get this sort of jolt, like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back more, just to get back on track. I know that probably sounds excessive… I just really can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight most of the day?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big thing of it, but when I look in the mirror I don’t really see what other people say they see. I just zero in on my stomach and my waist, and then it sort of sets the tone for the day.\n\nIf I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself a bit. If I don’t… it’s hard to think about much else. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re describing, where I can’t stop. I’m pretty controlled with food, honestly. If anything, I’d say I’m careful. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder because I just… I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nI know that may sound extreme. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just want to answer it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose objectively, yes. I mean, my uniform hangs off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, if that makes sense. When the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, part of me still thought, well, I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. Or at least I don’t see what other people seem to be seeing.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I tend to go straight to my stomach and my waist. If that looks… not lean enough, then it kind of colors the whole day. I know that probably sounds vain or unreasonable, but it’s more like I judge whether I can feel okay with myself based on that. Like whether I can respect myself that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is closely tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say it’s the only thing, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people tell me they see. I go straight to my stomach, my waist, whether I look lean enough. And if I don’t, it can make me feel like I’ve failed before the day’s even started.\n\nIt sounds a bit ridiculous saying it out loud, but yes… whether I can respect myself that day gets tied up in that.\n\nDoctor: Is fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think that’s probably the main thing. I’d like to say no, because that sounds… healthier, I suppose. But honestly, yes, it probably is.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.01\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… mostly I’m here because occupational health thought I should be checked out. They’re saying I’m not eating enough. I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most days I just have black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, and sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not ideal. It’s just that a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat a full meal?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m terrified, exactly. But yes, I do get worried. If the scale is up even half a pound, I get this sort of jolt, like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back more, just to get back on track. I know that probably sounds excessive… I just really can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight most of the day?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big thing of it, but when I look in the mirror I don’t really see what other people say they see. I just zero in on my stomach and my waist, and then it sort of sets the tone for the day.\n\nIf I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself a bit. If I don’t… it’s hard to think about much else. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re describing, where I can’t stop. I’m pretty controlled with food, honestly. If anything, I’d say I’m careful. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder because I just… I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nI know that may sound extreme. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just want to answer it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose objectively, yes. I mean, my uniform hangs off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, if that makes sense. When the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, part of me still thought, well, I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. Or at least I don’t see what other people seem to be seeing.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I tend to go straight to my stomach and my waist. If that looks… not lean enough, then it kind of colors the whole day. I know that probably sounds vain or unreasonable, but it’s more like I judge whether I can feel okay with myself based on that. Like whether I can respect myself that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is closely tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say it’s the only thing, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people tell me they see. I go straight to my stomach, my waist, whether I look lean enough. And if I don’t, it can make me feel like I’ve failed before the day’s even started.\n\nIt sounds a bit ridiculous saying it out loud, but yes… whether I can respect myself that day gets tied up in that.\n\nDoctor: Is fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think that’s probably the main thing. And then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder, telling myself I’m just being disciplined. I’d like to think I wouldn’t… if there were no mirror or number, maybe it would be easier.

But honestly, I think I probably still would. It’s not only seeing it. It’s this feeling that if I have a full meal, I’ve let my standards slip somehow. Most days I keep it pretty controlled—black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.

Occupational health says I’m not eating enough, which is partly why I’m here. I know that probably sounds worse when I say it out loud.

I wouldn’t want to make it sound dramatic, but most days I just have black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, and sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.\n\nI know that probably sounds… not ideal. It’s just that a full meal feels like I’m letting my standards slip, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight if you eat a full meal?\n\nPatient: I mean… I wouldn’t say I’m terrified, exactly. But yes, I do get worried. If the scale is up even half a pound, I get this sort of jolt, like I’ve let things get away from me. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back more, just to get back on track. I know that probably sounds excessive… I just really can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight most of the day?\n\nPatient: I suppose… yes, more than I’d like to admit. I try not to make a big thing of it, but when I look in the mirror I don’t really see what other people say they see. I just zero in on my stomach and my waist, and then it sort of sets the tone for the day.\n\nIf I look lean enough, I feel like I can respect myself a bit. If I don’t… it’s hard to think about much else. That sounds a bit vain, doesn’t it? I’m not trying to be difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, feeling like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, not in the way you’re describing, where I can’t stop. I’m pretty controlled with food, honestly. If anything, I’d say I’m careful. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself vomit. That’s not really something I do. I mean, I may feel uncomfortable after eating, but I don’t… do that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to control your weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean… I know what those are, but I don’t use them to control my weight. That’s not part of what I do.\n\nI try to be disciplined in other ways, but not with laxatives or water pills or anything like that. Is that… is that what you needed to know?\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, some. I didn’t really think of it as significant at first. My uniform does hang off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut when the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, I still kind of thought, well… I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably doesn’t sound right. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly impacted if you gained a small amount of weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to say no, that it wouldn’t matter. A small amount sounds small, right?\n\nBut honestly, if the scale is even up half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control somehow. Then I spend the rest of the day trying to cut back harder, because I can’t stand the idea that I’m putting weight back on.\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. I’m just trying to answer you accurately.\n\nDoctor: Have you had your menstrual period in the past few months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m male, so I don’t have menstrual periods. I’m not sure if that was just part of the standard questions, but… no, that wouldn’t apply to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting your food intake to avoid gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main reason, if I’m being honest.\n\nI tell myself it’s about discipline or staying sharp, but if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control. Then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder because I just… I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nI know that may sound extreme. I’m not trying to be difficult, I just want to answer it correctly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are currently underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I suppose objectively, yes. I mean, my uniform hangs off me now, and I’ve had to tighten my belt past the old notches.\n\nBut it’s hard for me to see it that way, if that makes sense. When the nurse said my weight was dangerously low, part of me still thought, well, I could stand to be a little sharper. I know that probably isn’t the answer you’d want me to have, but I’m trying to be honest.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you look underweight when you see yourself in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I guess… not really, no. Or at least I don’t see what other people seem to be seeing.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I tend to go straight to my stomach and my waist. If that looks… not lean enough, then it kind of colors the whole day. I know that probably sounds vain or unreasonable, but it’s more like I judge whether I can feel okay with myself based on that. Like whether I can respect myself that day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your self-worth is closely tied to your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI don’t know if I’d say it’s the only thing, but when I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what other people tell me they see. I go straight to my stomach, my waist, whether I look lean enough. And if I don’t, it can make me feel like I’ve failed before the day’s even started.\n\nIt sounds a bit ridiculous saying it out loud, but yes… whether I can respect myself that day gets tied up in that.\n\nDoctor: Is fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think that’s probably the main thing. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit… weak, or childish maybe.\n\nBut if the scale is up even half a pound, I get this jolt, like I’ve lost control of myself. And then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder, telling myself I’m just being disciplined. Really, I think it’s because I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the primary reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if the scale is even up a little — like half a pound — I get this awful jolt, like I’ve lost control.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back harder that day. I tell myself it’s just discipline, but… honestly, it’s because I can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal, even if others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say I’m underweight, and I’m not trying to argue with them exactly. I go straight to my stomach and my waist, and if they don’t look lean enough, then the whole day feels… off. Like I haven’t done well enough. And then I’ll spend the day cutting back harder, telling myself I’m just being disciplined. Really, I think it’s because I can’t stand the idea of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the primary reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it is. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but if the scale is even up a little — like half a pound — I get this awful jolt, like I’ve lost control.\n\nAnd then I’ll usually cut back harder that day. I tell myself it’s just discipline, but… honestly, it’s because I can’t stand the thought of putting weight back on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is normal, even if others tell you that you are underweight?\n\nPatient: I… I know people say I’m underweight, and I’m not trying to argue with them exactly. They’re probably seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, I don’t see it that way. I go straight to my stomach and my waist, and if they don’t look lean enough, then the whole day feels… off. Like I haven’t done well enough. I know that may not sound reasonable, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your perception of your body shape or weight is different from how others see you?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is different. I mean, I don’t want to sound like I’m ignoring what people are telling me, because I do hear it.\n\nBut when I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what they seem to see. My eyes go straight to my stomach and my waist, and if that area doesn’t look lean enough to me, it’s like… I judge the whole day by it. Whether I can feel okay with myself, or respect myself, almost. I know that may sound a bit extreme, but that’s honestly how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the most important aspect of your self-image?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, because that sounds… healthier, I suppose. But honestly, yes, it probably is.\n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don’t really see what everyone else says they see. I go straight to my stomach and my waist, and if I don’t look lean enough there, it affects how I feel about myself for the whole day. Like whether I can respect myself or not.\n\nI know that may sound excessive, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to restrict your food intake if you couldn't see yourself in the mirror or didn't know your weight?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think I wouldn’t… if there were no mirror or number, maybe it would be easier.\n\nBut honestly, I think I probably still would. It’s not only seeing it. It’s this feeling that if I have a full meal, I’ve let my standards slip somehow. Most days I keep it pretty controlled—black coffee, maybe a plain yogurt, sometimes a few bites of chicken at night.\n\nOccupational health says I’m not eating enough, which is partly why I’m here. I know that probably sounds worse when I say it out loud."
